alt="Professional marathon runners racing together at the 2025 Berlin Marathon">

The fastest marathon ever run was not a record.

In 2019, Eliud Kipchoge ran 1:59:40.2 in the Ineos run, with rotating pacers and lab-like conditions. It proved a human could break two hours. It was not a ratified race, so it never entered the record books.

Seven years later, the barrier fell for real. Sabastian Sawe ran 1:59:30 in London on April 26, 2026, in legal shoes, in a real race: ten seconds faster than the run that did not count.1

One treadmill test made running 4% cheaper.

Picture the setup: 18 elite men on a Colorado treadmill, running 14, 16, and 18 km/h in three mass-matched racing shoes. The Vaporfly prototype burned 4.16% less energy than the Nike Streak 6 and 4.01% less than the adidas Adios Boost 2. Both gaps cleared statistical muster (p<0.001).2

Hoogkamer et al. (2018) was funded by Nike, and two co-authors worked at the Nike Sport Research Lab; later independent analyses found the same direction of benefit.

Every runner in the study improved, which is striking. But look at who they were: fast men in their twenties, all wearing size 10, all on a treadmill. “Four percent” is a lab average, not a guarantee stamped on your receipt.

Four percent cheaper in the lab. What is that on the clock?

Make it personal: move the slider to your marathon best and watch the lab number turn into clock time. The verdict line reads it back in plain words.

2016 racing flat100
Vaporfly prototype95.84

Energetic cost in relative units, flat set to 100. Difference: −4.16%

≈2.6 to 3.4%modeled velocity gain
Your marathon in the Vaporfly prototype2:53:53 to 2:55:19Modeled time saved 4:41 to 6:07

Model estimate, not a guarantee. Kipp et al. convert a 4% economy gain into roughly 2.6 to 3.4% faster velocity at marathon pace; air resistance and individual response matter.3

Running economy is fuel burn.

Here is what the lab actually measured: the energy needed to hold one fixed speed. The prototype just made that pace cheaper to run.

The clock moves less.

You might be thinking: 4% cheaper, so 4% faster? Not quite. Kipp’s models translate the economy gain into roughly 2.6 to 3.4% more marathon speed.

Move the personal best.

Use the slider. The display applies that velocity band to a finishing time. It is a modeled range, not a performance prediction.

The street test pointed the same way.

The New York Times Upshot dug through thousands of paired Strava race records, including runners who raced the same events in different shoes. Runners in the new shoes ran 4 to 5% faster than in average shoes, and 1.5 to 2.5% faster than in the next-best shoe.4

It is observational: it cannot fully control training, weather, the course, who reports which shoe, or who even uses Strava. But it pointed the same direction as the lab.5

The record line broke when the shoes arrived.

Drag the record-year slider (or just scroll) and watch the marks fall. Look for the hollow dot at 1:59:40: a run that happened but was never a record.6

Men’s world-record progression, ratified marks only.

Pre-super-shoeVaporfly · 2017WA rules · 2020Current
SCROLL-DRIVEN RECORD LINE5 / 5
1:59:30

Sabastian Sawe · London · April 26, 2026 · first sub-2:00 marathon in a ratified race

2003 to 2014

The line barely moved.

Paula Radcliffe’s 2:15:25 stood for 16 years. Dennis Kimetto ran 2:02:57 in Berlin in 2014, back when shoes were just shoes.

2018 to 2023

Then the curve broke.

Then Kipchoge chipped the men’s mark down to 2:01:39 (2018) and 2:01:09 (2022). Kiptum took it to 2:00:35 in 2023; he died in February 2024 at age 24.

2019 · ghost run

1:59:40.2 was not a record.

Kipchoge’s 2019 Ineos run used rotating pacers and lab-like conditions. It proved a human could do it, but it was not a ratified race.

APRIL 26, 2026

The barrier fell for real.

Sawe ran 1:59:30 in London, with Kejelcha (1:59:41) and Kiplimo (2:00:28) right behind him. Three men under the old record in a single race.

The shoe is not the whole story.

World Athletics stepped in in January 2020, effective April 30, with road-shoe limits: 40 mm maximum stack and no more than one rigid plate. That July it set track-spike limits at 20 mm for events up to 800 m and 25 mm above 800 m. Kipchoge’s Ineos shoe was reported at roughly 50 mm with multiple plates, which would fail today’s limits outright. Current models must also sit on retail shelves for at least four months, and approved shoes can be inspected during record ratification.7

The plate got the name, but the foam may do as much work. The original prototype changed foam, stack height, plate, and geometry all at once, so the plate’s solo contribution was never cleanly isolated; reviews suggest the foam may contribute as much as the plate, or more. The 2016-to-2026 change was a whole system: lower stack and conventional foam with no plate became 40 mm of resilient foam, one curved plate, and rocker geometry.8

Not everyone gets four percent. A September 2026 summary compared the top 50 road performances from 2009 to 2015 against 2017 to 2024, using throwing events as an era-wide control: women’s performances improved 4.51% against 2.62% for men, with throws near 1%. Possibly women’s records simply had more room to move; that is an interpretation, not a settled biological effect.9

The evidence on injury points both ways. Worth watching: lower cadence and more rearfoot eversion in advanced footwear; five case reports of navicular bone stress injuries in elite runners; surveys report ankle, Achilles, and plantar-fascia symptoms, though surveys cannot prove causation. Reassuring: lower ankle plantarflexion moments in the same 2026 PM&R study of 23 elite runners; a 2025 study found reduced muscle and joint forces; no increase in Achilles loading and no consistent plate effect on joint power or leg stiffness. No published study has measured actual injury rates in super-shoe versus conventional-shoe cohorts.10

The shoes are proven to make you faster. Nothing has proven they break you, or that they do not.

1:59:30

Legal shoes. A real race. The revolution won.

Sawe’s 1:59:30 needed no asterisks: legal shoes, a real race, and ten seconds faster than Kipchoge’s unofficial Ineos run.

Superscript numbers in the essay point here. Each note gives the source and says plainly where the figure comes from.

  1. Record marks are from World-Athletics-recognized race reporting, current through October 2026. Kipchoge’s 1:59:40.2 (2019 Ineos run) used rotating pacers and is not a ratified record. Chepngetich’s 2:09:56 record stands; she was suspended in July 2025 after a failed drug test. Assefa’s women-only record is 2:15:41, London 2026. ↩
  2. Hoogkamer W et al. Sports Medicine. 2018;48(4):1009 to 1019. DOI 10.1007/s40279-017-0811-2. n=18 elite men; mass-matched shoes; 14/16/18 km/h on a treadmill; all wore size 10. ↩
  3. Kipp S, Kram R, Hoogkamer W. Frontiers in Physiology. 2019;10:79. DOI 10.3389/fphys.2019.00079. The 2.6 to 3.4% velocity band is model-dependent; air resistance and individual response matter. ↩
  4. Quealy K, Katz J. NYT Upshot, July 2018 and Dec. 13, 2019. Paired Strava race records; observational, self-selected runners. ↩
  5. Guinness J et al., arXiv:2002.06105 (2020). Independent analysis with photo-identified footwear. ↩
  6. Timeline dots are ratified marks only; the hollow dot is Kipchoge’s unofficial 1:59:40.2. Shoes are era classifications; model-by-model footwear is not always officially documented. ↩
  7. Jan. 31, 2020 amendment, effective Apr. 30; July 28, 2020 track amendment. Dates and dimensions are secondary-sourced from reporting of the World Athletics amendments; the current official rulebook was not checked. ↩
  8. Mechanism synthesis: Hoogkamer W, Kipp S, Kram R. Sports Medicine. 2019;49:133 to 143. DOI 10.1007/s40279-018-1024-z. The plate’s solo contribution was never cleanly isolated in the original experiment. ↩
  9. Sept. 2026 MarathonHandbook summary comparing top-50 road performances (2009 to 2015 vs 2017 to 2024) with throwing events as a control. The full paper has not been checked; preliminary. ↩
  10. Concern: Bruneau MM, Gaudette LW, et al. PM&R. Apr. 23, 2026. DOI 10.1002/pmrj.70153. n=23 (11 women, 12 men); biomechanical proxies, not injuries. Counter-evidence: Scientific Reports. 2025;15:17828. DOI 10.1038/s41598-025-03029-0. No published study has measured actual injury rates in super-shoe versus conventional-shoe cohorts. ↩
  11. Real professional runners at the 2025 Berlin Marathon, Wikimedia Commons; linked in the caption. No generated athlete or shoe imagery is used. ↩

Methods

Lab figures come from Hoogkamer et al. (2018): 18 elite men, treadmill, mass-matched shoes. The 2.6 to 3.4% velocity band is Kipp et al.’s model (2019), not a measurement. Street figures come from the NYT Upshot’s paired-Strava analysis (2018, 2019) and Guinness et al.’s photo-identified study (2020); both are observational. Records are World-Athletics-recognized marks through October 2026. Rules come from secondary reporting of the 2020 World Athletics amendments. The women’s 4.51% vs 2.62% figures come from a September 2026 MarathonHandbook summary whose full paper has not been checked. Injury evidence is biomechanical proxies, five case reports, and surveys; no cohort injury-rate study exists. The converter multiplies your entered time by the velocity band; it models a range, not a prediction. The record line connects ratified marks only, and the hollow dot is Kipchoge’s unofficial 1:59:40.2. Women’s records are shown as two separate series, mixed races and women-only races, because World Athletics records them separately; the women’s marks are drawn as discrete events, not one line. Chart data: data/carbon-plate-revolution/.
